Proof.

The proof is analogous to Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Step 2 of Theorem B]
, but is included in this paper for completeness.

The lack of focus-focus points implies
B=B_{\mathrm{wt}}
, thus
B
inherits a
\mathbb{Z}
-affine structure. By Corollary
4.5
,
B
is contractible, so there exists a developing map
f\colon B\cong\tilde{B}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By definition of the
\mathbb{Z}
-affine structure on
B
, since the first integral
J
of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
is the moment map of an effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action, one can choose the above developing map to be of the form
f(x,y)=\big(x,f^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
for some smooth function
f^{(2)}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}
. Fix such a choice.

To show that
f
is the required cartographic homeomorphism, it suffices to show that
f
is injective. If
f(x_{0},y_{0})=f(x_{1},y_{1})
, one gets immediately
x_{0}=x_{1}
. The map
f^{(2)}(x_{0},\cdot)\colon B\cap\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{0}\}\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly monotone as
\frac{\partial f^{(2)}}{\partial y}
does not vanish on
B
, because
f
is locally a diffeomorphism. This implies that
y_{0}=y_{1}
as required.

Since
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
is faithful semitoric and because of the form of
f
,
(M,\omega,f\circ\Phi)
is faithful semitoric. In fact, it is toric because
f\circ\Phi\colon(M,\omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the moment map of an effective Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{2}
-action. By Corollary
4.6
,
(M,\omega,f\circ\Phi)
is visible toric and by Corollary
2.45
,
f(B)
is locally convex.
∎

Intermezzo: classification of faithful semitoric systems with no focus-focus points via their cartographic images.

Lemma
5.2
allows one to classify faithful semitoric systems with no focus-focus points up to isomorphisms of integrable systems. This is achieved by understanding their sets of cartographic pairs and corresponding images.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(M,\omega,\Phi)
be a faithful semitoric system with no focus-focus points, let
B
denote its moment map image and fix a cartographic pair
(f,B)
for
(M,\omega,\Phi)
as in Lemma
5.2
. The set of cartographic pairs of
(M,\omega,\Phi)
whose second component is
B
is

\displaystyle\{(h\circ f,B)\,|\,h\in\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})\}.

Proof.

Corollary
2.58
implies that, for any
h\in\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
,
(h\circ f,B)
is also a cartographic pair for
(M,\omega,\Phi)
. Conversely, suppose that
(\hat{f},B)
is a cartographic pair for
(M,\omega,\Phi)
. The map
\hat{f}\circ f^{-1}\colon f(B)\to\hat{f}(B)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-affine isomorphism, where
f(B)
and
\hat{f}(B)
are endowed with the restriction of the standard
\mathbb{Z}
-affine structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since
f(B)
and
\hat{f}(B)
are connected, it follows that there exists
h\in\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
such that
h=\hat{f}\circ f^{-1}
, as desired.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

With the hypotheses of Lemma
5.3
, a cartographic homeomorphism
(\hat{f},B)
of
(M,\omega,\Phi)
is of the form given by equation (
5.1
) if and only if the element
h\in\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
constructed in the above proof belongs to the subgroup
\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
consisting of
\mathbb{Z}
-affine transformations that fix all vertical lines in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see Remark
4.13
).

Cartographic pairs of
(M,\omega,\Phi)
whose second component is
B
are henceforth referred to as being
maximal
and so are the corresponding cartographic images (see Remark
2.57
). Before stating the classification of faithful semitoric systems with no focus-focus points (up to isomorphisms of integrable systems, see Lemma
5.5
), observe that the number of focus-focus points is an invariant of the isomorphism class of a faithful semitoric system (see Section
3.2
).

Lemma 5.5
.

For
i=1,2
let
(M_{i},\omega_{i},\Phi_{i})
be faithful semitoric systems with no focus-focus points. Then
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
are isomorphic as integrable
systems if and only if their sets of maximal cartographic images are equal.

Proof.

Suppose first that
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
are isomorphic as integrable systems. Then Corollary
2.62
implies that the set of maximal cartographic images of the former is included in that of the latter. Reversing the above argument, we obtain the desired equality. Conversely, suppose that their sets of maximal cartographic images are equal. Then, for
i=1,2
, there exists a cartographic pair
(f_{i},B_{i})
with the property that
f_{1}(B_{1})=f_{2}(B_{2})
. Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3
imply that, for
i=1,2
,
(M_{i},\omega_{i},f_{i}\circ\Phi_{i})
are visible toric systems; moreover, the above assumption gives that their moment map images are equal. By Proposition
2.44
, this implies that there exists a symplectomorphism
\Psi\colon(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to(M_{2},\omega_{2})
such that
f_{2}\circ\Phi_{2}\circ\Psi=f_{1}\circ\Phi_{1}
. Set
\psi:=f_{2}^{-1}\circ f_{1}\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}
. The map
\psi
is a diffeomorphism since both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are diffeomorphisms and it satisfies
\Phi_{2}\circ\Psi=\psi\circ\Phi_{1}
. Thus
(\Psi,\psi)
is an isomorphism between
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
as desired.
∎

Using Lemma
5.5
, it is possible to deduce the classification of faithful semitoric systems with no focus-focus points up to any notion of isomorphism given in Definition
4.5
(see Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Lemma 3.3]
for a proof of the classification of proper semitoric systems according to the finest isomorphism type).

Cartographic homeomorphisms for faithful semitoric systems with at least one focus-focus point.
Let
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
be a faithful semitoric system with
B=\Phi(M)
that contains at least one focus-focus point. An example is
shown in Fig.
5.1
. We introduce vertical ‘cuts’ at the focus-focus values, along what Symington
[
43
]
refers to as ‘eigenrays’ in the more general context of (faithful) almost-toric systems. Our terminology is motivated by Vũ Ngọc
[
47
]
.

Figure 5.1:
The image of the moment map (gray) with the focus-focus values (marked by
\star
) and their projection onto the first component.

Let
B_{\mathrm{ff}}\subset\operatorname{Int}(B)
denote the set of focus-focus values. By Lemma
3.30
, it is a countable subset. To order the elements of
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
we fix the following convention for the indexing set of
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
. Set

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{\sup}:=\sup\{\operatorname{pr}_{1}(c)\,|\,c\in B_{\mathrm{ff}}\},\qquad\mathsf{x}_{\inf}:=\inf\{\operatorname{pr}_{1}(c)\,|\,c\in B_{\mathrm{ff}}\},

(5.2)

where
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is projection onto the first component. By property
(F5)
, this supremum
\mathsf{x}_{\sup}
(respectively infimum
\mathsf{x}_{\inf}
) is either attained as a maximum (respectively as a minimum) or does not lie in
J(M)
. Set

\displaystyle I:=\begin{cases}\{1,2,\ldots,\lvert B_{\mathrm{ff}}\rvert\}&\text{if }\lvert B_{\mathrm{ff}}\rvert<\infty,\\
\{1,2,\ldots\}&\text{if }\lvert B_{\mathrm{ff}}\rvert=\lvert\mathbb{N}\rvert\text{ and }\mathsf{x}_{\inf}\in J(M),\\
\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}&\text{if }\lvert B_{\mathrm{ff}}\rvert=\lvert\mathbb{N}\rvert\text{ and }\mathsf{x}_{\sup}\in J(M),\\
\mathbb{Z}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(5.3)

By construction, the cardinality of
I
equals that of
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
and thus we think of the elements of the latter as being indexed by
I
. Order the elements of
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
as follows. For
i\in I
, set
c_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
. Then require that
i<j
implies either
x_{i}<x_{j}
, or
x_{i}=x_{j}
and
y_{i}<y_{j}
; moreover, if
0,1\in I
, require that
x_{0}<x_{1}
. (If
I=\mathbb{Z}
, the above ordering is unique up to the choice of which focus-focus value is labeled with
0
.)

For each
i\in I
choose a sign
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{+1,-1\}
, and denote the associated vertical
cut
in
B
at
c_{i}
by

\displaystyle l^{\varepsilon_{i}}:=\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,x=x_{i},\,\varepsilon_{i}y\geq\varepsilon_{i}y_{i}\big\}\cap B.

When
\varepsilon_{i}=+1
(respectively
-1
), the cut
l^{\varepsilon_{i}}
is simply the intersection of
B
with the vertical half-line starting at
c_{i}
going ‘up’ (respectively ‘down’), see Fig.
5.2
. Therefore the former is referred to as being
upward
, while the latter as being
downward
. For a fixed
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
, denote the union of the cuts by
l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and set
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=B\smallsetminus l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Moreover, to each element
(x,y)\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}=B\smallsetminus B_{\mathrm{ff}}
,
associate the integer

\displaystyle j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y):=\sum\limits_{\{i\in I\,|\,(x,y)\in l^{\varepsilon_{i}}\}}\varepsilon_{i},

with the convention that
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=0
for
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Finiteness of
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
follows from property
(F6)
and Proposition
4.18
. The quantity
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
is a signed count of the number of cuts that pass through
(x,y)
, where upward cuts are counted positively and downward cuts negatively.

Figure 5.2:
Image of the moment map (gray) with cuts emanating from the focus-focus values (marked by
\star
). The choice of cuts in (a) and (b) leads to a simply connected set whereas the choice in (c) yields two connected components.

Corollary 5.6
.

The subset
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is open and dense in
B
.

Proof.

Density of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
in
B
is trivial, so it remains to prove its openness in
B
and, to this end, it suffices to prove that
l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is closed in
B
. Let
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}\subset l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be a sequence that converges to
(x_{0},y_{0})\in B
. This implies that the sequence
\{x_{n}\}=\operatorname{pr}_{1}(\{(x_{n},y_{n})\})\subset J(M)
converges to
x_{0}\in J(M)
. By construction and by Proposition
4.18
,
\{x_{n}\}
is contained in the subset of critical values of
J
, which does not contain any limit points in
J(M)
by property
(F5)
. Therefore, for all but finitely many
n
,
x_{n}=x_{0}
, which, in turn, implies that
(x_{n},y_{n})\in\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{0}\}
for all but finitely many
n
. By property
(F6)
and Proposition
4.18
, the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{0}\}
contains finitely many focus-focus values and, therefore, finitely many cuts. Seeing as each cut is a closed subset, then the union of all cuts contained on
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{0}\}
is closed. Therefore,
(x_{0},y_{0})\in l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
as required.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

In general, it is not true that
l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is closed in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for
\mathsf{x}_{\sup},\mathsf{x}_{\inf}
may belong to
\mathbb{R}\smallsetminus J(M)
.

The notation
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is suggestive of the fact that there exists a cartographic homeomorphism
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is a cartographic pair for
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
. Before stating and proving the precise existence statement (Theorem
5.11
below), we prove some further properties of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
(see Fig.
5.2
).

Lemma 5.8
.

The subset
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected if and only if
\varepsilon_{i}\geq\varepsilon_{j}
for all
i>j
with
x_{i}=x_{j}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected and let
i>j\in I
be such that
x_{i}=x_{j}
. Let
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be points with
x_{1}<x_{i}<x_{2}
. Such points exist because focus-focus values are contained in
\operatorname{Int}(B)
. Since
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected, there exists a path in
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
starting at
(x_{1},y_{1})
and ending at
(x_{2},y_{2})
. Therefore, there exists a point
(x_{i},y^{\prime})\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and thus
\varepsilon_{i}\geq\varepsilon_{j}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
satisfies the condition that
\varepsilon_{i}\geq\varepsilon_{j}
for all
i>j
with
x_{i}=x_{j}
. First we show that, for all
\mathsf{x}_{1}\in J(M)
,
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\}\cap S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\neq\varnothing
and that the set is path-connected. If
\mathsf{x}_{1}\notin\operatorname{pr}_{1}(B_{\mathrm{ff}})
, one obtains

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\}\cap S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\}\cap B

and the result follows from the fact that
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
is a faithful semitoric system. Suppose, therefore, that
\mathsf{x}_{1}\in\operatorname{pr}_{1}(B_{\mathrm{ff}})
. By property
(F6)
and Proposition
4.18
, there are finitely many focus-focus values
(\mathsf{x}_{1},y_{i_{1}}),\ldots,(\mathsf{x}_{1},y_{i_{N}})
lying on the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\}
. Set

\displaystyle y_{+}:=\inf\{y_{i_{k}}\,|\,\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=+1\},\qquad y_{-}:=\sup\{y_{i_{k}}\,|\,\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=-1\}.

Since
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
satisfies the condition in the statement, it follows that
y_{+}>y_{-}
and therefore,

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{1}\}\cap S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\,,\,y_{+}>y>y_{-}\},

which shows that
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}_{1}\}\cap S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected. By Corollary
5.6
,
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is open in
B
. Thus
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
satisfies all the hypotheses of Proposition
4.7
and, therefore, the subsystem of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
relative to
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is faithful semitoric. By Corollary
4.5
,
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is
contractible and, in particular, path-connected.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

The subset
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected if and only if it is contractible.

Corollary 5.10
.

There exists a choice of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
making
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
path-connected.

Proof.

The choice of
\varepsilon_{i}=+1
for all
i\in I
satisfies the condition of Lemma
5.8
.
∎

Having established the above preliminary results, we can state and prove existence of cartographic homeomorphisms for faithful semitoric systems.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
be a faithful semitoric system with
B_{\mathrm{ff}}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}\neq\varnothing
. For any
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
, there exists a cartographic pair
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
, where
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=B\smallsetminus l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is the complement of the vertical cuts determined by
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

is of the form
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\big(x,f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
, satisfying the following properties

(C1)

for all
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, the quantity
\operatorname{sgn}\big(\frac{\partial f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}{\partial y}(x,y)\big)=:\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is
constant;

(C2)

for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x<\mathsf{x}}}}Df_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})&1\end{pmatrix}\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x>\mathsf{x}}}}Df_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y).

In particular,
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)
is locally convex.

Any cartographic homeomorphism
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying the properties in Theorem
5.11
is said to be
associated
to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
.

The proof of Theorem
5.11
is split into two cases: when
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected and when it is not.

Proof of Theorem
5.11
if
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected.

A choice of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
whose associated set
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected exists by Corollary
5.10
. Suppose that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected. The idea is to argue as in Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Steps 2–4 in the proof of Theorem B]
, recalling and adjusting the argument therein as much as necessary for our purposes.

By construction
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset B_{\mathrm{wt}}
. Now let
\mathsf{q}\colon\tilde{B}_{\mathrm{wt}}\to B_{\mathrm{wt}}
denote the universal covering. By Corollary
5.9
,
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is contractible and, in particular, simply connected. Therefore, there exists a smooth section
\sigma\colon S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\to\tilde{B}_{\mathrm{wt}}
of
\mathsf{q}
. Consider a developing map
\mathrm{dev}\colon\tilde{B}_{\mathrm{wt}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
constructed by fixing basepoints
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in\sigma(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
. Set
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=\mathrm{dev}\circ\sigma\colon S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.2
, it is possible to choose
\mathrm{dev}
so that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\big(x,f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
for any
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Fix such a choice. Following the arguments in Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Step 4 of the proof of Theorem B]
,
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
can be extended to an embedding
B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
which, by abuse of notation, is also denoted by
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. By construction and by density of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset B
,
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is a cartographic pair with
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\big(x,f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)\big)
. Thus for all
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
,
\frac{\partial f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}{\partial y}(x,y)\neq 0
. Since
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected, property
(C1)
follows.

To complete the proof, there are two cases to consider, depending on whether
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=+1
or
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=-1
. In the first case, property
(C2)
and local convexity of
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)
can be proved as in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Steps 5 and 6 of the proof of Theorem 3.8]
. Thus suppose that
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=-1
. Setting
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
,
\big(\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)
is a cartographic pair which can be constructed as above satisfying
\operatorname{sgn}(\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=+1
. (This corresponds to adjusting the above choice of developing map by composing on the left with the map
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
; see Remark
5.4
.) Fix
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
. Then, using property
(C2)
for
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and the fact that
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=-1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x<\mathsf{x}}}}Df_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x<\mathsf{x}}}}D\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})&1\end{pmatrix}\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x>\mathsf{x}}}}D\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x>\mathsf{x}}}}D\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})&1\end{pmatrix}\lim_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(x,y)\to(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})}}{{x>\mathsf{x}}}}Df_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y),

This proves property
(C2)
in general.

Finally, observe that
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)
is locally convex and that
\mathbb{Z}
-affine maps preserve this property. Thus
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)
is locally convex as required.
∎

Now we turn to the case of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
not beingpath-connected. There exist proofs for such cases in the literature (see for instance Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Step 5 of the proof of Theorem B]
). The argument presented below, however, uses different techniques.

Before delving into the proof, we introduce some useful notions and notation. For any
\mathsf{x}\in J(M)
, set

\displaystyle N_{\mathsf{x}}:=\lvert\{i\in I\,|\,x_{i}=\mathsf{x}\}\rvert.

By property
(F6)
and Proposition
4.18
,
N_{\mathsf{x}}
is finite for any
\mathsf{x}\in J(M)
. Moreover, for a fixed
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
, set

\displaystyle N_{\mathsf{x}}^{\pm}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\pm\lvert\{i\in I\,|\,x_{i}=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1\}\rvert.

Observe that, for any
\mathsf{x}\in J(M)
and any
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
,

\displaystyle N_{\mathsf{x}}=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})-N_{\mathsf{x}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}).

Moreover, for any
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
and any
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
,

\displaystyle j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})+N_{\mathsf{x}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}).

Fix
\mathsf{x}\in J(M)
with
N_{\mathsf{x}}\neq 0
. Then, by property
(F6)
and Proposition
4.18
, there exist finitely many indices
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{N_{\mathsf{x}}}
in
I
with
x_{i_{j}}=\mathsf{x}
. Observe that, by definition of the ordering on
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
,

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\}\cap B_{\mathrm{wt}}\subset\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y<y_{i_{1}}\}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\cup\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{N_{\mathsf{x}}-1}\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y_{i_{j}}<y<y_{i_{j+1}}\}\cup\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y>y_{i_{N_{\mathsf{x}}}}\}.

Lemma 5.12
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
and for all
\mathsf{x}\in J(M)
with
N_{\mathsf{x}}\neq 0
, the function
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\cdot)\colon\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\}\cap B_{\mathrm{wt}}\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfies

•

j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y<y_{i_{1}}\}
;

•

j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})+k=N^{+}_{\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})-N_{\mathsf{x}}+j
for all
k=1,\ldots,N_{\mathsf{x}}-1
and for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in\{(x,y)\,|

x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y_{i_{k}}<y<y_{i_{k+1}}\}
;

•

j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }y>y_{i_{N_{\mathsf{x}}}}\}
.

Hereby,
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{N_{\mathsf{x}}}
are the elements of
I
such that
x_{i_{j}}=\mathsf{x}
. In particular, the function
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\cdot)
only depends on
N_{\mathsf{x}}^{\pm}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

Fix
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
and consider
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
such that
N_{\mathsf{x}}\neq 0
. Suppose first that
\mathsf{y}<y_{i_{1}}
. This means that
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
is ‘below’ all focus-focus values on the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\}
. By definition of the ordering on
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
and of the cuts associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
, for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,N_{\mathsf{x}}\}
, if
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=-1
then
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in l^{\varepsilon_{i_{k}}}
, while if
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=+1
, then
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\notin l^{\varepsilon_{i_{k}}}
. Thus

\displaystyle j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=-\lvert\{i\in I\,|\,x_{i}=\mathsf{x}\text{ and }\varepsilon_{i}=-1\}\rvert=N^{-}_{\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})

as required. Similarly, if
\mathsf{y}>y_{i_{N_{\mathsf{x}}}}
, then
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=N^{+}_{\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
, for
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
is ‘above’ all focus-focus
values on the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=\mathsf{x}\}
.

It remains to prove the intermediate cases for which we proceed by induction on
k
. The base case is
\mathsf{y}<y_{i_{1}}
, which has already been proved. Suppose that the required statement holds for all
m<k
and let
y_{i_{k}}<\mathsf{y}<y_{i_{k+1}}
. Set, for any
(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
,

\displaystyle j^{\pm}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}}):=\sum\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\in I\,,\,(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})\in l^{\varepsilon_{i}},}}{{\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1}}}\varepsilon_{i}.

Clearly, for any
(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
,
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})=j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{+}(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})+j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{-}(\bar{\mathsf{x}},\bar{\mathsf{y}})
. Fix some
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}^{\prime})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
with
y_{i_{k-1}}<\mathsf{y}^{\prime}<y_{i_{k}}
. The inductive hypothesis implies
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}^{\prime})=N_{\mathsf{x}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})+k-1
. There are two cases to consider, depending on whether
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=+1
or
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=-1
. In the former case, observe that
j^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}^{\prime})+1
, while
j^{-}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j^{-}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}^{\prime})
. Thus,
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}^{\prime})+1=N^{-}_{\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})+k
as required. The latter case is proved analogously, swapping the roles of
j^{+}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
and
j^{-}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
.
∎

With the above results at hand, we finish the proof of Theorem
5.11
.

Proof of Theorem
5.11
if
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is not path-connected.

Suppose that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is not path-connected. The idea is to reduce this situation to the path-connected case by appealing to the following result.

Lemma 5.13
.

There exists a unique
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
such that

•

S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
;

•

for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
,
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
;

•

S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is path-connected.

Assume Lemma
5.13
, whose proof is below, and let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be as in Lemma
5.13
. Let
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Set
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
. Since
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
, Corollary
5.6
implies that
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is a cartographic pair; property
(C1)
holds by construction. Property
(C2)
holds because Lemma
5.13
implies that
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})
holds by definition. Local convexity of
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)=f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(B)
is also true as
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is associated to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
in the sense of Proposition
5.11
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
5.11
for the case that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is not path-connected.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.13
.

For a fixed
x_{i}
, the map
y\mapsto j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x_{i},y)
is an integer-valued map whose image is all integers between (and including)
N^{-}_{x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
and
N^{+}_{x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
. Moreover, it is easily seen that the above map is constant along the open segments lying between consecutive focus-focus values. Therefore there exists a segment on the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
along which
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x_{i},\cdot)
is equal to zero. Consider
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\{+1,-1\}^{I}
that is equal to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
except for those indices corresponding to focus-focus values lying on
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
; for those indices, set
\bar{\varepsilon}_{j}=\pm 1
according to whether
c_{j}
lies above or below the segment on which
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x_{i},\cdot)
is equal to zero. It can be checked that, for all
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
,
j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j_{\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
. Performing this operation for all
x_{i}
yields
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
satisfying the requirements of Lemma
5.8
for
S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
to be path-connected. Moreover, by construction,
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
and it can be easily checked that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is the unique choice of signs that satisfies all the above properties.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The above argument for the case of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
not being path-connected only works because the system
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
satisfies property
(F7)
. If focus-focus values of higher multiplicity are allowed, then there may be no analogue of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
as in Lemma
5.13
. In this case, the issue is that there is a choice of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
such that there exists an
x_{i}
for which there is no interval on which the function
y\mapsto j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x_{i},y)
is zero (see Fig.
5.3
).

Figure 5.3:
The focus-focus value
c
(displayed by
\star
) has multiplicity two and the two signs associated to
c
are
+1
and
-1
.

Remark 5.15
.

The argument proving the case of Theorem
5.11
when
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is not path-connected shows that, for a given faithful semitoric system, the set of all cartographic homeomorphisms that satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
5.11
equals that of the cartographic homeomorphisms associated to those
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
for which
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected (cf. Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Proposition 4.1]
).

To finish this section, we prove Proposition
4.14
using Theorem
5.11
.

Proof of Proposition
4.14
.

Let
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
be a faithful semitoric system with at least one focus-focus point. If
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
is a faithful semitoric system isomorphic to
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
as an integrable system, then the cardinality of the set of focus-focus values of the former equals that of the set of focus-focus values of the latter, as the isomorphism induces a bijection between the focus-focus values. In order to prove the result, the non-trivial implication to check is that if
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1}=(J_{1},H_{1}))
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2}=(J_{2},H_{2}))
are isomorphic as integrable systems, then they are isomorphic as complexity one systems. In fact, we aim to achieve more: we show that any isomorphism
(\Psi,\psi)
as integrable systems between
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1}=(J_{1},H_{1}))
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2}=(J_{2},H_{2}))
is necessarily an isomorphism as complexity one systems.

Fix an isomorphism
\big(\Psi,\psi=\big(\psi^{(1)},\psi^{(2)}\big)\big)
as integrable systems between
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1}=(J_{1},H_{1}))
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2}=(J_{2},H_{2}))
, let
I
denote the indexing set of the set of focus-focus values
B_{1,\mathrm{ff}}
and set
B_{1,\mathrm{ff}}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. Begin by observing that the function
\psi^{(1)}\circ\Phi_{1}\colon M_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
is the moment map of an effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action that is system-preserving. This is because
\big(\Psi,\psi=\big(\psi^{(1)},\psi^{(2)}\big)\big)
is an isomorphism of integrable systems and because
J_{2}
is the moment map of an effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action that preserves the integrable system
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Fix a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
so that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset B_{1}
is path-connected (Corollary
5.10
ensures that such a choice exists), and fix a cartographic homeomorphism
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\colon B_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
as in Theorem
5.11
. The above observation implies that the map
\psi^{(1)}\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}
is smooth and
\mathbb{Z}
-affine. Since
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is connected and endowed with the restriction of the standard
\mathbb{Z}
-affine structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it follows that there exist
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
(x,y)\in f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
,
\psi^{(1)}\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\zeta_{1}x+\zeta_{2}y+a
. As
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\subset f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B_{1})
is dense, it follows that, for all
(x,y)\in f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B_{1})
,
\psi^{(1)}\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\zeta_{1}x+\zeta_{2}y+a
. Therefore, for all
(x,y)\in B_{1}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{(1)}(x,y)=\zeta_{1}x+\zeta_{2}f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)+a,

(5.4)

since
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\big(x,f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(x,y)\big)
. To prove the result, it suffices to show that
\zeta_{1}\in\{+1,-1\}
and that
\zeta_{2}=0
. To this end, fix
i\in I
and let
U_{i}
be an open neighborhood of
c_{i}
in
B_{1}
with the property that the subsystem of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1}=(J_{1},H_{1}))
relative to
U_{i}
is a saturated regular neighborhood of the focus-focus fiber
\Phi^{-1}_{1}(c_{i})
, i.e., all fibers in
\Phi^{-1}_{1}(U_{i})
are regular except for the focus-focus fiber
\Phi^{-1}_{1}(c_{i})
(see Section
3.2
). Since
\psi^{(1)}\circ\Phi_{1}|_{\Phi_{1}^{-1}(U_{i})}
is the moment map of an effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action that is system-preserving, Proposition
3.23
implies that, on
\Phi^{-1}_{1}(U_{i})
,
\pm dJ_{1}=d\big(\psi^{(1)}\circ\Phi_{1}\big)
. Since
\Phi_{1}
is a submersion away from the focus-focus point
p_{i}
lying on
\Phi^{-1}_{1}(c_{i})
and
\Phi_{1}\big(\Phi^{-1}_{1}(U_{i})\smallsetminus\{p_{i}\}\big)=U_{i}
, it follows that, on
U_{i}
,

\displaystyle d\psi^{(1)}=\pm dx.

(5.5)

On the other hand, formula (
5.4
) implies that, on
B_{1}
,

\displaystyle d\psi^{(1)}=\left(\zeta_{1}+\zeta_{2}\frac{\partial f^{(2)}}{\partial x}\right)dx+\zeta_{2}\frac{\partial f^{(2)}}{\partial y}dy.

(5.6)

Comparing (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) and using the fact that
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
are constant and that
\frac{\partial f^{(2)}}{\partial y}\neq 0
on
B_{1}
, we have that
\zeta_{2}=0
and
\zeta_{1}=\pm 1
, as desired.
∎

5.2
The set of cartographic pairs

Given a faithful semitoric system
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
, it is natural to ask for a description of the set of all cartographic pairs of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
that satisfy the properties in Theorem
5.11
. These cartographic pairs and their images are henceforth referred to as
semitoric
. One reason why it is important to describe the set of semitoric cartographic images of a faithful semitoric system with at least one focus-focus point is that it is an invariant of the isomorphism class of the system as a strict complexity one system (see Definition
4.12
).

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
be faithful semitoric systems that are isomorphic as strict complexity one spaces and suppose that
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
has at least one focus-focus point. Then the sets of semitoric cartographic images of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
are equal.

Proof.

Let
(\Psi,\psi)
be an isomorphism as strict complexity one spaces between faithful semitoric systems
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
, i.e.,
\psi(x,y)=\big(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
for some smooth function
\psi^{(2)}\colon B_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
. First, observe that, since
(\Psi,\psi)
is, in particular, an isomorphism as integrable systems, it induces a bijection between the sets of focus-focus values of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
. In what follows, this bijection is used tacitly to identify the indexing sets
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
of the sets of focus-focus values of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Suppose that
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
is a semitoric cartographic pair for
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Corollary
2.62
implies that
\big(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi,\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})\big)
is a cartographic pair for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
. The aim is to show that this pair is semitoric, i.e., that

(1)

there exists a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}
for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
such that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}=\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
, and

(2)

the map
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}:=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi
is associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}
, i.e., as per the hypotheses of Theorem
5.11
,
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
is of the form
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(x,y)=\big(x,f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(x,y)\big)
for some continuous function
f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}\colon B_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
, and it satisfies properties
(C1)
and
(C2)
.

Begin by observing that, since
\psi(x,y)=\big(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
, the preimage of a vertical line under
\psi
is a vertical line. This implies that the preimage of the vertical cuts determined by
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}
are vertical half-lines. Moreover, since
(\Psi,\psi)
is an isomorphism of integrable systems, the ‘starting point’ of the above vertical lines are focus-focus values of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
. This determines uniquely a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}
for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
such that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}=\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
, thus proving
(1)
. To prove
(2)
, observe that, by definition,
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi
. Since
\psi(x,y)=\big(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
, the fact that
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
satisfies the properties of Theorem
5.11
implies that

\displaystyle f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(x,y)=\big(x,f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\big(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)\big)=:\big(x,f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(x,y)\big).

Using the chain rule, for any
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}}{\partial y}(x,y)=\frac{\partial f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}}{\partial z}(x,z)\bigg\rvert_{z=\psi^{(2)}(x,y)}\frac{\partial\psi^{(2)}}{\partial y}(x,y).

(5.7)

Observe that, by construction,
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
if and only if
(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y))\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
. Moreover, since
\psi(x,y)=\big(x,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
is a diffeomorphism and
B_{1}
is connected, the quantity
\operatorname{sgn}\big(\frac{\partial\psi^{(2)}}{\partial y}(x,y)\big)

=:\operatorname{sgn}(\psi)
does not depend on
(x,y)\in B_{1}
. Using the fact that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
satisfies property
(C1)
, equation (
5.7
) yields that
\operatorname{sgn}\big(\frac{\partial f^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}}{\partial y}(x,y)\big)=:\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
does not depend on
(x,y)\in S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
. In fact,
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})=\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})\operatorname{sgn}(\psi)
. This shows that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
satisfies property
(C1)
. To see that it satisfies property
(C2)
, observe that, for any
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{1,\mathrm{wt}}
,

\displaystyle j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})=j_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}(\psi(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}))\operatorname{sgn}(\psi).

Using the above formula, the chain rule and the fact that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
satisfies property
(C2)
, it can be shown that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
satisfies property
(C2)
as well. Thus
\big(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi,\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})\big)
is a semitoric cartographic pair for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
, as desired. Hence, the set of semitoric cartographic images of
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
is contained in that of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
. Reversing the roles of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
completes the proof.
∎

Providing a description of the set of semitoric cartographic images of a faithful semitoric system with at least one focus-focus point is the aim of this section, which generalizes, while being heavily inspired by, work of Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Section 4]
. Henceforth, fix a faithful semitoric system
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
with at least one focus-focus point. The idea is to show that any cartographic homeomorphism of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
as in Theorem
5.11
can be constructed from a fixed one by means of composing on the left with a suitable homeomorphism (see Corollary
5.17
and Theorem
5.24
for precise statements). It is convenient to consider two separate cases:

•

Determine all cartographic homeomorphisms associated to a given choice of signs (Corollary
5.17
).

•

Determine how cartographic homeomorphisms associated to possibly distinct choices of signs are related (Theorem
5.24
).

First, we consider the set of all cartographic homeomorphisms associated to a given choice of signs; this is described in the following result, which is analogous to Remark
5.4
. Recall that
\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
denotes the subgroup of
\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
that preserves vertical lines (see Remark
4.13
).

Corollary 5.17
.

Fix a faithful semitoric system
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
and a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
. If
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are cartographic homeomorphisms associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
, then there exists an element
h\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
with
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Conversely, for any
h\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
, the map
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is a cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

It may be assumed without loss of generality that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is path-connected since the not path-connected case can be reduced to the path-connected one as in the proof of Theorem
5.11
(see Remark
5.15
). Let
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be cartographic homeomorphisms associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
. Then their restrictions to
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are developing maps for the induced
\mathbb{Z}
-affine structure on
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Therefore, arguing as in Remark
5.4
, there exists an element
h\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
with
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
. Since
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset B
is dense, this implies that
\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
as required.

Conversely, the proof of Theorem
5.11
gives that composing a cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
on the left with an element of
\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
yields another cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Having established Corollary
5.17
, we study the problem of relating cartographic homeomorphisms whose associated signs are not necessarily equal. Before stating the main result of this section we introduce some tools akin to those needed in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Section 4]
, but slightly more involved as faithful semitoric systems allow for the presence of infinitely many focus-focus points (see Remarks
5.18
,
5.19
and
5.25
below).

As in Section
5.1
, let
I
be the indexing set of the set of focus-focus values
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
defined in equation (
5.3
). Also, fix the ordering on
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
as in the paragraph following equation (
5.3
), so elements of
B_{\mathrm{ff}}
are denoted by
c_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
for
i\in I
. Henceforth, fix elements
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
and associated cartographic homeomorphisms
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Furthermore, fix a basepoint
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
with the property that
x_{0}<\mathsf{x}<x_{1}
. (If
x_{0}
or
x_{1}
is not defined, then only the other inequality is required.)

Remark 5.18
.

The above choice of basepoint agrees with that made in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, proof of Proposition 4.1]
. However, there is an important difference that arises because of the possibility of having infinitely many focus-focus points for faithful semitoric systems. In what follows we must allow for the case in which there are focus-focus values ‘to the left’ of the basepoint, i.e., with notation as above, for the case in which there exists
i\in I
with
x_{i}<\mathsf{x}
. (If this is the case, then by the choices of indexing set
I
of equation (
5.3
) and of basepoint, there are infinitely many such indices.)

Throughout this section, set
T:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. For any
i\in I
, set

\displaystyle k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}):=\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{i}-\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}}{2}\right).

Moreover, for any
i\in I
define
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows:

•

If
i\leq 0
, let
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
be the identity.

•

If
i>0
, let
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
be the piece-wise
\mathbb{Z}
-affine transformation that acts as the identity on the half-space
x<x_{i}
and as the shear
T^{k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}
on the half-space
x\geq x_{i}
.

Analogously, for any
i\in I
define
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows:

•

If
i\leq 0
, let
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
be the piece-wise
\mathbb{Z}
-affine transformation that acts as the shear
T^{-k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}
on the half-space
x<x_{i}
and as the identity on the half-space
x\geq x_{i}
.

•

If
i>0
let
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
be the identity.

Remark 5.19
.

While the maps
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
are those used in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Section 4]
, the maps
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
are needed in the following precisely because of the possibility of focus-focus values existing ‘to the left’ of the basepoint (see Remark
5.18
).

Explicitly, we have that if
i>0
then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}:=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}&\text{if }x<x_{i},\\
\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})x_{i}\end{pmatrix}\right)&\text{if }x\geq x_{i},\end{cases}

and if
i\leq 0
then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
k_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})x_{i}\end{pmatrix}\right)&\text{if }x\leq x_{i},\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{if }x>x_{i}.\end{cases}

The maps
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
are well-defined and satisfy the following properties, the proofs of which are left to the reader.

Lemma 5.20
.

For each positive
(
respectively non-positive
)

i\in I
, the map
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}

(
respectively
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})
is a homeomorphism that fixes the vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
pointwise and is a
\mathbb{Z}
-affine isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\smallsetminus\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

For any
i,j\in I
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{l}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}},\qquad\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}},\qquad\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{j,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}.

One ingredient in the construction of the homeomorphism that relates
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is the composition of the maps
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
or
\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
as
i
ranges over all indices in
I
. Seeing as this may involve the composition of infinitely many maps different from the identity (as
I
may be infinite), some care is needed. To this end, we first introduce notation for the domains of these possibly infinite compositions. Set

\displaystyle D_{\sup}:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{2}&\text{if }\mathsf{x}_{\sup}\in J(M),\\
\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,x<\mathsf{x}_{\sup}\big\}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle D_{\inf}:=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{2}&\text{if }\mathsf{x}_{\inf}\in J(M),\\
\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,x>\mathsf{x}_{\inf}\big\}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{x}_{\sup},\mathsf{x}_{\inf}\in\mathbb{R}
are defined as in equation (
5.2
). Observe that
B\subset D_{\sup}\cap D_{\inf}
.

Next, we define the desired compositions. If the cardinality of
I
is finite, the situation is entirely analogous to the one considered in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Section 4]
. For, with the above conventions,
I
being finite implies that
\mathsf{x}_{\sup},\mathsf{x}_{\inf}\in J(M)
and that
I
only contains positive elements. In this case, set
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\sup}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\inf}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
to be equal to the finite compositions
\mathfrak{l}_{\lvert I\rvert,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\lvert I\rvert-1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{l}_{1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\lvert I\rvert,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{\lvert I\rvert-1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}
respectively. Observe that the latter is, by definition, equal to the identity. It remains to consider the case in which
I
is infinite. If
I
has infinitely many positive elements, set, for any
(x,y)
with
x\leq x_{i}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(x,y):=\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{i-1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{l}_{1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}(x,y).

Analogously, if
I
has infinitely many non-positive elements, set, for any
(x,y)

with
x\geq x_{i}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(x,y):=\mathfrak{r}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{i+1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{0,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}(x,y).

In the remaining cases, set
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathrm{id}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
. The following result is implied by the fact that any given point is only acted upon by finitely many elements of the composition (cf. Pelayo, Ratiu and Vũ Ngọc
[
36
, Lemma 2.1]
).

Lemma 5.22
.

The maps
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\sup}\to D_{\sup}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\inf}\to D_{\inf}
are well-defined.

In fact, more is true.

Lemma 5.23
.

The maps
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\sup}\to D_{\sup}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon D_{\inf}\to D_{\inf}
are homeomorphisms that are
\mathbb{Z}
-affine isomorphisms away from the set
\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
.

Proof.

The only non-trivial cases to consider are those of
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
if
I
contains infinitely many positive elements and of
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
if
I
contains infinitely many non-positive elements. Consider the former case (the latter is entirely analogous). To see that
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is a homeomorphism, observe that the proof of Lemma
5.22
implies that, for all
(x,y)\in D_{\sup}
,
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(x,y)=\big(x,\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
, for some continuous function
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}^{(2)}\colon D_{\sup}\to\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, it can be checked directly that, for any
x<\mathsf{x}_{\sup}
, the function
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}^{(2)}(x,\cdot)
is strictly increasing. Therefore,
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is a homeomorphism onto its image. Since for any
i\geq 1
the map
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{i-1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{l}_{1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}
sends
\{(x,y)\,|\,x\leq x_{i}\}
onto itself, it follows that
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(D_{\sup})=D_{\sup}
. The fact that it is a
\mathbb{Z}
-affine isomorphism away from
\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{i}\}
follows from the fact that, for any
i\geq 1
,
\mathfrak{l}_{i,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{i-1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{l}_{1,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}
also satisfies this property (see Lemma
5.20
).
∎

With the maps
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
at hand, we can state the main result of this section. (Recall that
\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
denotes the subgroup of
\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
preserving vertical lines, see Remark
4.13
).

Theorem 5.24
.

Let
(M,\omega,\Phi\!=\!(J,H))
be a faithful semitoric system. Given any
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\!\in\!\{{+}1{,}{-}1\}^{I}
and any two cartographic homeomorphisms
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
respectively, there exists a transformation
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
such that

\displaystyle f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}.

The main idea behind Theorem
5.24
is not new; it first appears in Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Proposition 4.1]
in the context of
semitoric systems. However, the more general context of faithful semitoric systems, where there may be infinitely many focus-focus points, deserves to be dealt with carefully. For instance, the transformation
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
, which is not needed in the study of semitoric systems, is necessary in this context (see Remarks
5.18
and
5.19
).

Remark 5.25
.

In fact, the statement and proof of Theorem
5.24
may be of use in the study of semitoric systems as well. The main references for these systems make the underlying (tacit) assumption that the signs of the cartographic homeomorphisms, as in Theorem
5.11
, are positive (cf. Pelayo and Vũ Ngọc
[
38
,
39
,
47
]
).

Proof of Theorem
5.24
.

The proof is split into three steps:

Step 1:

Construct the map
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
.

Step 2:

Reduce to the simpler case in which
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in precisely one component.

Step 3:

Prove the simpler case.

Step 1: constructing the map
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
.
As above, fix a basepoint
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\in B_{\mathrm{wt}}
with the property that
x_{0}<\mathsf{x}<x_{1}
. (If
x_{0}
or
x_{1}
is not defined, then only the other inequality is required.) Denote by
\mathfrak{S}
the connected component of
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cap S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
containing
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
. Since both
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
are open, so is
\mathfrak{S}
. Moreover it is path-connected by definition. Furthermore, it can be checked that
\mathfrak{S}
intersects any vertical line either in an empty or in a connected set. Therefore, by Proposition
4.7
, the subsystem of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
relative to
\mathfrak{S}
is faithful semitoric and contains
(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
. Moreover, by construction, it contains no focus-focus value. By Lemma
2.60
, the maps
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
and
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
are cartographic homeomorphisms for the subsystem of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
relative to
\mathfrak{S}
. Therefore, by Remark
5.4
, there exists
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
such that
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{S})}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
. The map
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
is the desired one.

Step 2: reducing to a simpler case.
Observe that, by Corollary
5.17
, the map
h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is a cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, the above argument shows that
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=(h_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})|_{\mathfrak{S}}
. Thus, in order to prove the result in the statement of the theorem, it suffices to prove that, if
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
, then
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. Henceforth, assume that
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
, which implies
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})=\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
. In fact, we can simplify the argument further: it suffices to prove the claimed result under the assumption that all but one of the components of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are equal. For, if the latter holds, we can argue as follows. Consider a sequence of choices of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}
, for
s\in\{1,2,\ldots\}\cup\{\infty\}
, such that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\infty}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, and, for any
s\geq 1
, all but one component of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}
are equal. Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in finitely components, say in
r
components, choose the above sequence so that for all
s\geq r+1
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. For each
s\geq 1
, fix a choice of cartographic homeomorphism
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}
with the property that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
. Moreover, require that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\infty}}=f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
, and that, if
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in precisely
r
components, then for all
s\geq r+1
,
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}=f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
.
Using the above sequence of signs and associated cartographic homeomorphisms and the fact that the claimed result holds when all but one component of the signs are equal, we obtain, for all
s\geq 1
, maps
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}
satisfying

\displaystyle f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}.

(5.8)

Therefore, iterating equation (
5.8
), for all
s\geq 1
,

\displaystyle f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s-1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s-1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},

(5.9)

where we use the fact that
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
. If
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in precisely
r
components, then, by construction, for all
s\geq r+1
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}=\mathrm{id}=\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{s+1}}.

Therefore, in this case, equation (
5.9
) yields that

\displaystyle f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r+1}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r+1}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r-1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r-1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}.

Because the homeomorphisms in the above composition commute (see Lemma
5.21
), by definition of
\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
,

\displaystyle f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}.

Thus the result is proved if
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in finitely many components.

The case in which they differ by infinitely many components is entirely analogous, as we can consider the composite of infinitely many maps of the above form on, say, any compact subset of
B
(see the proof of Lemma
5.22
) and use a compact exhaustion of
B
. Thus, assuming that the result holds when the choices of signs differ in precisely one component, the result holds in general.

Step 3: proving the simple case.
Assume that
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
and that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in precisely one component. Under these assumptions the result can be proved exactly as in
Vũ Ngọc
[
47
, Proposition 4.1]
, whose key ideas are explained below. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ precisely in the
i
th component. By Corollary
2.63
,
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{-1}
is a homeomorphism that is piecewise
\mathbb{Z}
-affine. Using the proof of Theorem
5.11
, it may be assumed without loss of generality that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
are path-connected. As both sets are dense, it suffices to check the desired equality on
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cap S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
differ in precisely one component, it follows that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cap S_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
has two connected components,
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, which are open and satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
4.7
. Thus the subsystems of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
relative to
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
are faithful semitoric. By Lemma
2.60
, the restrictions of
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
to
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
are cartographic homeomorphisms for the respective subsystems of
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
. By construction, these subsystems contain no focus-focus points. Thus, Remark
5.4
implies that there exist
h_{\mathfrak{S}},h_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{Vert}(2;\mathbb{Z})
with
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=h_{\mathfrak{S}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
and
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}}=h_{\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}}
.

By assumption,
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
, so
h_{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathrm{id}
; on the other hand, the above assumptions imply that
(\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})|_{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathrm{id}
. Thus the desired equality holds on
\mathfrak{S}
. It remains to check that it does on
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Using property
(C2)
for
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
and
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
and the fact that
\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\operatorname{sgn}(f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})
, it can be shown that the linear parts of
h_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}
and of
(\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})|_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}
are equal. To see that their translational components are equal, observe that the piecewise
\mathbb{Z}
-affine transformation given on
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{S})
and on
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime})
by
h_{\mathfrak{S}}
and
h_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}
, respectively, extends uniquely to a topological embedding of
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(B)
onto
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(B)
(which equals
f_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{-1}
). In particular, it acts as the identity on the vertical line containing the
i
th focus-focus value. This implies that the translational component of
h_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}
equals that of
(\mathfrak{r}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\circ\mathfrak{l}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}})|_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}
.
∎

To conclude this section, we enlarge the set of semitoric cartographic images of a faithful semitoric system with at least one focus-focus point to construct an invariant of the system up to isomorphisms of
integrable systems
. To this end, recall that
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
is the subset consisting of elements of the form

\displaystyle\left(\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}&0\\
k&\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\end{pmatrix}\right),

where, for
i=1,2
,
\eta_{i}\in\{+1,-1\}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
(see Remark
4.13
). Given a faithful semitoric system
(M,\omega,\Phi)
with at least one focus-focus point, let
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
be one of its semitoric cartographic pairs. By Lemma
2.58
, given any element
\mathsf{l}\in\mathsf{L}
,
(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is a cartographic pair for
(M,\omega,\Phi)
. Cartographic pairs of the form
(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
, where
\mathsf{l}\in\mathsf{L}
and
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is a semitoric cartographic pair, are said to be
complexity one
, and so are their images. The set of complexity one images of
(M,\omega,\Phi)
is an invariant of the system up to isomorphisms of integrable systems.

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
be faithful semitoric systems that are isomorphic as integrable systems and suppose that
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
has at least one focus-focus point. Then the sets of complexity one cartographic images of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
are equal.

Proof.

Let
(\Psi,\psi)
be an isomorphism of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
as integrable systems. Since
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
has at least one focus-focus point, Proposition
4.14
implies that
(\Psi,\psi)
is an isomorphism as complexity one spaces, i.e.,
\psi(x,y)=\big(\eta x+a,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
for some
\eta\in\{\pm 1\}
and some
a\in\mathbb{R}
(see Definition
4.12
). For an arbitrary
\mathsf{l}\in\mathsf{L}
and a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}
that make
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\subset B_{2}
connected (a choice that exists by Corollary
5.10
), consider the complexity one cartographic pair
(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
for
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.16
,
\big(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi,\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})\big)
is a cartographic pair for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
. Moreover, since
\psi
is of the above form, there exists a unique choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}
for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
such that
\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})=S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
. Since
\psi
is a diffeomorphism and
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
is connected, so is
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
. Let
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}\colon B_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}
as in Theorem
5.11
. Observe that, by construction
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
is connected. By Corollary
2.63
, the restriction of the map
\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
to
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-affine isomorphism onto
\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
. Since
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
is connected, there exists an element
h\in\mathrm{AGL}(2;\mathbb{Z})
such that
h=\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
on
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
. As
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})\subset f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(B_{1})
is dense, it follows that
h=\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi\circ f^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
on
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}(B_{1})
or, equivalently,
h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}=\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi
. Moreover, observe that
h\in\mathsf{L}
, for
\mathsf{l}\in\mathsf{L}
,
(f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}})
are semitoric cartographic pairs and
\psi(x,y)=\big(\eta x+a,\psi^{(2)}(x,y)\big)
. This shows that the cartographic pair
\big(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\circ\psi,\psi^{-1}(S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})\big)=(h\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}})
for
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
is complexity one. Since the set of images of complexity one cartographic pairs
(\mathsf{l}\circ f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}},S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})
such that
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
is connected equals the set of images of complexity one cartographic pairs of
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
(see Remark
5.15
), this shows that the set of complexity one cartographic images of
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
is contained in that of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
. Reversing the roles of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
in the above argument completes the proof.
∎

5.3
\boldsymbol{\eta}
-cartographic faithful semitoric systems

Let
(M,\omega,\Phi)
be a faithful semitoric system. The presence of a focus-focus point implies that no faithful semitoric system isomorphic to
(M,\omega,\Phi)
has a cartographic moment map (see Section
2.7
). On the other hand, Theorem
5.11
provides cartographic homeomorphisms associated to choices of vertical cuts. Fix any such cartographic homeomorphism
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
; while it is tempting to think of
(M,\omega,f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\circ\Phi)
as an integrable system, the lack of smoothness of
f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
prevents it from being one. (If we were to adopt the non-standard convention of Harada and Kaveh
[
18
, Definition 2.1]
,
(M,\omega,f_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\circ\Phi)
would be an integrable system.) The aim of this section is to show that, in some sense, the next best scenario holds: Given a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
with
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
connected, any cartographic homeomorphism associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
can be modified in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of the cuts associated to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
so that it becomes everywhere smooth (see Theorem
5.36
for a precise statement). This smoothing of cartographic homeomorphisms generates representatives in the isomorphism class of a faithful semitoric system, which we call
\boldsymbol{\eta}
-cartographic
, that are particularly useful when defining surgeries on (isomorphism classes of) faithful semitoric systems (cf. the forthcoming
[
22
]
). (The above sentence holds for the finest notion of isomorphism of faithful semitoric systems given by Definition
4.12
and, therefore, for all.) Moreover, we show that cartographic homeomorphisms are, in some sense, limits of what we call
\boldsymbol{\eta}
-cartographic embeddings
(see Proposition
5.40
).

Throughout the rest of this section, a faithful semitoric system
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
containing at least one focus-focus singular point is fixed. (Recall that
(M,\omega,\Phi=(J,H))
is required to satisfy the generic assumption
(F7)
throughout.) As above, set
B=\Phi(M)
, let
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset\operatorname{Int}(B)
denote the set of focus-focus values of
(M,\omega,\Phi)
, where
I
is ordered as in equation (
5.3
), while
l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
denotes the union of the vertical cuts in
B
associated to a choice of signs
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in\{+1,-1\}^{I}
. Also let
S_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=B\smallsetminus l^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
denote the complement of those cuts. Finally, assume a cartographic homeomorphism has positive sign (see Theorem
5.11
) unless otherwise stated.

5.3.1
Admissible half-strips for faithful semitoric systems

First, we define the (closed) neighborhoods of vertical half-lines that we use to construct the smoothing of a given cartographic homeomorphism.

Definition 5.27
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\{+1,-1\}
,
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\eta>0
, and a continuous map
\gamma\colon\big[x_{0}-\frac{\eta}{2},x_{0}+\frac{\eta}{2}\big]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\varepsilon y_{0}>\varepsilon\gamma(x)
for all
x\in\big[x_{0}-\frac{\eta}{2},x_{0}+\frac{\eta}{2}\big]
. A
half-strip centered at
(x_{0},y_{0})
of sign
\varepsilon
and width
\eta
with bounding curve
\gamma
is the following closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\eta,\gamma}^{\varepsilon}(x_{0},y_{0}):=\left\{(x,y)\,|\,x_{0}-\frac{\eta}{2}\leq x\leq x_{0}+\frac{\eta}{2}\text{ and }\varepsilon y\geq\varepsilon\gamma(x)\right\}

(see Fig.
5.4
). The vertical line
\{(x,y)\,|\,x=x_{0}\}
is called the
center line
of the half-strip. When the center point
(x_{0},y_{0})
and the bounding curve
\gamma
